### Chapter 3 {#alfonso-gran-mezzo.1.3}

**THE CONDITIONS OF PRAYER.**

#### 1. {#alfonso-gran-mezzo.1.3.1}

**Which are the Requisite Conditions.**

#### OBJECT OF PRAYER.

_ Amen, amen, I say to you, if you ask the Father anything in My name, He will give it you.’ Jesus Christ then has promised, that whatever we ask his Father in his name, his Father will give us. But always with the understanding that we ask under the proper conditions. Many seek, says St. James, and obtain not, because they seek improperly: Ye ask and receive not, because ye ask amiss.[^3-65-2] So St. Basil, following out the argument of the Apostle, says, “ You sometimes ask and receive not, because you have asked badly; either without faith, or you have requested things not fit for you, or you have not persevered;” * “ faithlessly,” that is, with little faith, or little confidence; “lightly,” with little desire of the grace you ask; “things not fit for you,” when you seek good things that will not be conducive to your salvation; or you have left off praying, without perseverance. Hence St. Thomas reduces to four the conditions required in prayer, in order that it may produce its effect: [Grimm III, 66][^3-66-4][^3-66-3][^3-66-2][^3-66-1] these are, that a man asks (1) for himself; (2) things necessary for salvation; (3) piously; and (4) with perseverance.’ \

#### CAN WE PRAY EFFICACIOUSLY FOR OTHERS ?

The first condition then of prayer is, that you make it for yourself; because St. Thomas holds, that one man cannot ex condigno (7. e. in the fitness of things) obtain for another eternal life; nor, consequently, even those graces which are requisite for his salvation. Since, as he says, the promise is made not to others, but only to those that pray: “He shall give to you.” Nevertheless, there are many theologians, Cornelius 4 Lapide, Sylvester, Tolet, Habert, and others, who hold the opposite doctrine, on the authority of St. Basil, who teaches that prayer, by virtue of God’s promise, is infallibly efficacious, even for those for whom we pray, provided they put no positive impediment in the way. And they support their doctrine by Scripture: Pray one for another, that you may be saved; for the continual prayer of the just man availeth much? Pray for them that persecute and calumniate you.’ And better still, on the text of St. John: He that knoweth his brother to sina sin which is not to death, let him ask, and life shall be given to him who sinneth not unto death. There ts a sin unto death; for that I say not that any man ask. St. Ambrose, St. Augustine, the Ven. Bede, and others,’ explain the words “who sinneth not unto death” to mean, provided the sinner is not one who intends to remain obstinate till death; since for sucha one a very extraordin- [Grimm III, 67] ry grace would be required. But for other sinners, who re not guilty of such malice, the Apostle promises their onversion to him who prays for them: Let him ask, and fe shall be given him for him that sinneth.

#### WE OUGHT TO PRAY FOR SINNERS.,

Besides, it is quite certain that the prayers of others re of great use to sinners, and are very pleasing to God; nd God complains of his servants who do not recomnend sinners to him, as he once complained to St. Mary Magdalene of Pazzi, to whom he said one day: “See, my laughter, how the Christians are in the devil’s hands; f my elect did not deliver them by their prayers they vould be devoured.” But God especially requires this f priests and religious. The same saint used to say to er nuns: “ My sisters, God has not separated us from the yorld, that we should only do good for ourselves, but so that we should appease him in behalf of sinners,” nd God one day said to her, “I have given to you my hosen spouses the City of Refuge (i.e., the Passion of ‘esus Christ), that you may have a place where you may ybtain help for my creatures. Therefore have recourse 0 it, and thence stretch forth a helping hand tomy creaures who are perishing, and lay down your lives for hem.” For this reason the saint, inflamed with holy eal, used to offer God the blood of the Redeemer fifty imes a day in behalf of sinners, and was quite wasted way for the desire she had for their conversion. Oh, she used to say, what pain is it, O Lord, to see how one ould help Thy creatures by giving one’s life for them, ind not be able to do so! For the rest, in every exercise he recommended sinners to God; and it is written in her ife, that she scarcely passed an hour in the day without praying for them. Frequently, too, she arose in the niddle of the night, and went to the Blessed Sacrament [Grimm III, 68]

t 68 The Conditions of Prayer. (PART I to pray for them; and yet for all this, when she was once found bathed in tears, on being asked the cause, she answered, “ Because I seem to myself todo nothing foi the salvation of sinners.’”’ She went so far as to offer te endure even the pains of hell for their conversion, provided that in that place she might still love God; and often God gratified her by inflicting on her grievous pains and infirmities for the salvation of sinners. She prayed especially for priests, seeing that their good life was the occasion of salvation to others, while their bad life was the cause of ruin to many; and therefore she prayed God to visit their faults upon her, saying, “ Lord, make me die and return to life again as many timesas is necessary to satisfy Thy justice for them!” And it is related in her life, that the saint, by her prayers, did indeed release many souls from the hands of Lucifer.

I wished to speak rather particularly of the zeal of this Saint; but, indeed, no souls that really love God neglect to pray for poor sinners. For how is it possible for a person who loves God, and knows what love he has for our souls, and what Jesus Christ has done and suffered for their salvation, and how our Saviour desires us ta pray for sinners,—how is it possible, I say, that he should be able to look with indifference on the numbers of poor souls who are living without God, and are slaves of hell, without being moved to importune God with frequent prayers to give light and strength to these wretched beings, so that they may come out from the miserable state of living death in which they are slumbering? True it ig that God has not promised to grant our requests, whem those for whom we pray puta positive impediment in the way of their conversion; but still, God of his goodness has often deigned, at the prayer of his servants, to bring back the most blinded and obstinate sinners to a state of salvation, by means of extraordinary graces. Therefore let us never omit, when we say or hear Mass, when we [Grimm III, 69] eceive Holy Communion, when we make our meditation rr our visit to the Blessed Sacrament, to recommend poor inners to God. And a learned author says, that he who ays for others will find that his prayers for himself are eard much sooner. But this is a digression. Let us iow return to the examination of the other conditions hat St. Thomas lays down as necessary to the efficacy if prayer.

#### WE MUST ASK FOR GRACES NECESSARY TO SALVATION.

The second condition assigned by the saint is, that we sk those favors which are necessary to salvation; beause the promise annexed to prayer was not made with eference to temporal favors, which are not necessary for he salvation of the soul. St. Augustine, explaining the vords of the Gospel, “whatever ye shall ask in my lame,” says, that “nothing which is asked in a way derimental to salvation is asked in the name of the Savour.”! Sometimes, says the same Father, we seek some emporal favors, and God does not hear us; but he does ot hear us because he loves us, and wishes to be merciul tous. “A man may pray faithfully for the necessiies of this life, and God may mercifully refuse to hear ‘im ; because the physician knows better than the patint what is good for the sick man.” * The physician who oves his patient will not allow him to have those things hat he sees would do himharm. Oh, how many, if they ad been sick or poor, would have escaped those sins vhich they commit in health and in affluence! And, herefore, when men ask God for health or riches, he of- 1<*Non petitur in nomine Salvatoris, quidquid petitur contra raionem salutis.”—/ Jo. tr. 102. 2 ‘‘Fideliter supplicans Deo pro necessitatibus hujus vite, et miseriorditer auditur, et misericorditer non auditur; quid enim infirmo sit tile, magis novit medicus quam egrotus.”—Ap. s. Prosp. Sent. 212. a -sitmnarrwy cr ALARV'C CALILECCE [Grimm III, 70][^3-70-2][^3-70-1]

: o ten denies them because he loves them, knowing that these things would be to them an occasion of losing his grace, or at any rate of growing tepid in the spiritual? life. Not that we mean to say that it is any defect to pray to God for the necessaries of this present life, so far as they are not inconsistent with our eternal salvation, as the Wise man said: Give me only the necessaries of life.’ Nor is it a defect, says St. Thomas,’ to have anxiety about such goods, if it is not-inordinate. The defect. consists in desiring and seeking these temporal goods, and in having an inordinate anxiety about them, as if they were our highest good. Therefore, when we ask of God these temporal favors, we ought always to ask them with resignation, and with the condition, if they will be useful to our souls; and when we see that God does not grant them, let us be certain that he then denies them to us for the love he bears us, and because he sees that they would be injurious to the salvation of our souls. ;

It often happens that we pray God to deliver us from some dangerous temptation, and yet that God does not hear us, but permits the temptation to continue troubling us. In such a case, let us understand that God permits, even this for our greater good. It is not temptation or bad thoughts that separate us from God, but our con= sent to the evil. Whena soulin temptation recommends itself to God, and by his aid resists, oh, how it then advances in perfection, and unites itself more closely to God! and this is the reason why God does not hear it. St. Paul prayed instantly to be delivered from the temptation of impurity: Zhere was given me a sting of my flesh an angel of Satan to buffet me; for which thing thrice I be- [Grimm III, 71][^3-71-2][^3-71-1] sought the Lord, that it might depart from me. But God answered him, that it was enough to have his grace: My grace is‘sufficient for thee. So that even in temptations we ought to pray with resignation, saying, Lord, deliver me from this trouble, if it is expedient to deliver me; and if not, at least give me help toresist. And here comes in what St. Bernard says, that when we beg any grace of God, he gives us either that which we ask, or some other thing more useful to us. He often leaves us to be buffeted by the waves, in order to try our faithfulness, and for our greater profit. It seems then that he is deaf to our prayers. But no; let us be sure that God then really hears us, and secretly aids us, and. strengthsens us by his grace to resist all the assaults of our enemies. See how he himself assures us of this by the mouth of the Psalmist: Thou calledst upon me tn affliction, and I deavered thee: I heard thee in the secret place of tempest; 1 proved thee at the waters of contradiction.”

#### OTHER CONDITIONS OF PRAYER.

The other conditions assigned by St. Thomas to prayer ire, that it is to be made piously and perseveringly; by siously, he means with humility and confidence—by perseveringly, continuing to pray until death. We must 10w speak distinctly of each of these three conditions, which are the most necessary for prayer, namely of hunility, confidence, and perseverance. [Grimm III, 72][^3-72-6][^3-72-5][^3-72-4][^3-72-3][^3-72-2][^3-72-1]

#### 2. {#alfonso-gran-mezzo.1.3.2}

The Humility with which we should Pray. ‘ ¢

The Lord does indeed regard the prayers of his servants, but only of his servants who are humble. He hath had regard to the prayer of the humble’ Others he does not regard, but rejects them: God resisteth the proud, and giveth grace to the humble.” He does not hear the prayers of the proud who trust in their own strength; but for that reason leaves them to their own feebleness; and in this state, deprived of God’s aid, they must certainly perish. David had to bewail this case: Before J was humbled I offended.* I sinned because I was not humble. The same thing happened to St. Peter, who, though he was warned by our Lord that all the disciples would abandon Him on that night—A4Z you shall be scandalised in Me this night *— nevertheless, instead of acknowledging his own weak. ness, and begging our Lord’s aid against his unfaithful-_ ness, was too confident in his own Strength, and said, that though all should abandon him he would never leave him: Although all shall be scandéalised in T, hee, I will” never be scandalised® And although our Saviour again foretold to him, in a special manner, that in that very | night, before the cock-crow, he should deny him three times; yet, trusting in his own courage, he boasted, Saying, Yea, though I should die with T, hee, I will not deny . Thee.* But what came of it? Scarcely had the unhappy man entered the house of the high priest, when he was _ accused of being a disciple of Jesus Christ, and three [Grimm III, 73][^3-73-4][^3-73-3][^3-73-2][^3-73-1] | The Humility with which we should Era 73 times did he deny with an oath that he had ever knowa him: And again he denied with an oath, that I know not the Man. If Peter had humbled himself, and had asked our Lord for the grace of constancy, he would not have denied him.

We ought all to feel that we are standing on the edge of a precipice, suspended over the abyss of all sins, and supported only by the thread of God’s grace. If this thread fails us, we shall certainly fall into the gulf, and shall commit the most horrible wickedness. Unless the Lord had been my helper, my soul had almost dwelt in hell. If God had not succored me, I should have fallen into a thousand sins, and now I should be in hell. So said the Psalmist, and so ought each of us to say. This is what St. Francis of Assisi meant, when he said that he was the worst Sinner in the world. But, my Father, said his companion, what you say is not true; there are many in the world who are certainly worse than you are. Yes, what I say is but too true, answered St. Francis; because if God did not keep his hand over me, I should commit every possible sin.

It is of faith, that without the aid of grace we cannot do any good work, nor even think a good thought. “Without grace men do no good whatever, either “in thought or in deed,” says St. Augustine.” As the eye cannot see without light, so, say the holy Father, man can’ do no good without grace. The Apostle had said the same thing before him: Wot that we are sufficient to think anything of ourselves, as of ourselves ; but our suffictency is of God. And David had said it before St. Paul: Un- [Grimm III, 74][^3-74-7][^3-74-6][^3-74-5][^3-74-4][^3-74-3][^3-74-2][^3-74-1] less the Lord build the house, they labor in vain that build it.’ In vain does man weary himself to become a saint, unless” God lends a helping hand: Unless the Lord keep the city, he” watcheth in vain that keepeth it.” lf God did not preserve the soul from sins, in vain will it try to preserve itself by its own strength: and therefore did the holy prophet protest, J will not trust in my bow.’ I will not hope in my arms; but only in God, who alone can save me.

Hence, whoever finds that he has done any good, and does not find that he has fallen into greater sins than those which are commonly committed, let him say with St. Paul, By the grace of God I am what I am ;* and for the same reason, he ought never to cease to be afraid of falling on every occasion of sin: Wherefore, he that thinketh himself to stand, let him take heed lest he. fall. St. Paul wishes to warn us that he who feels secure of not falling, is in great danger of falling; and he assigns the reason in another place, where he says, Z/ any man think himself to be something, whereas he ts nothing, he deceiveth himself. q So that St. Augustine wrote wisely, “the presumption of stability renders many unstable; no one will be so firm as he who feels himself infirm.”’ If a man says he has no fear, it is a sign that he trusts in himself, and in his good resolutions; but such a man, with his mischievous confidence, deceives himself, because, through trust in his own strength, he neglects to fear; and through not [Grimm III, 75] fearing he neglects to recommend himself to God, and then he will certainly fall. And so, for like reasons, we should all abstain from noticing with any vain-glory the sins of other people; but rather we should then esteem ourselves as worse in ourselves than they are, and should say, Lord, if thou hadst not helped me, I should have done worse. Otherwise, to punish us for our pride, God will permit us to fall into worse and more shameful sits. For this cause St. Paul instructs u: to labor for our salvation; but how? always in fear and trembling: With fear and trembling work out vour salvation.’ Ves; Morice who has a great fear of falling, distrusts his own strength, and therefore places his confidence in God, and will have recourse to him in dangers; and God will aid him, and so he will vanquish his temptations, and will be saved. St. Philip Neri, walking one day through Rome, kept saying, “I am in despair!” A certain religious rebuked him, and the saint thereupon said, “My father, I am in despair for myself; but I trust in God.” So must we do, if we would be saved; we must always live in despair of doing anything by our own strength; and in so doing we shall imitate St. Philip, who used to say to God the first moment he woke in the morning, “ Lord, keep Thy hands over Philip this day; for if not, Philip will betray Thee.”

This, then, we may conclude with St. Augustine, is all the grand science of a Christian,—to know that he is nothing, and can do nothing. “ This is the whole of the great science, to know that man is nothing.”* For then he will never neglect to furnish himself, by prayer to God, with that strength which he has not of himself, and which he needs in order to resist temptation, and to do good; and so, with the help of God, who never refuses anything to the man who prays to him in humility, he .

2 «Hee est tota scientia magna, scire quia homo nihil est,”—/u Ps. lxx. s. I. [Grimm III, 76][^3-76-2][^3-76-1] will be able to do all things: Zhe prayer of him that humbleth himself shall pierce the clouds, and he will not depart, until the Most High behold.” The prayer of an humble soul penetrates the heavens, and presents itself before the throne of God; and departs not without God's looking on it and hearing it. And though the soul be guilty of any amount of sin, God never despises a heart that humbles itself: A contrite and humble heart, O God, Thou wilt not despise ;* God reststeth the proud, but giveth grace to the humble. As the Lord is severe with the proud, and resists their prayers, so is he kind and liberal to the humble. This is precisely what Jesus Christ said one _day to St. Catharine of Sienna: ‘Know, my daughter, that a soul that perseveres in humble prayer gains every | virtue.” * ] It will be of use to introduce here the advice which the learned and pious Palafox, Bishop of Osma, gives to spiritual persons who desire to become saints. It occurs in a note to the 18th letter of St. Teresa, which she wrote to her confessor, to give him an account of all the grades of supernatural prayer with which God had favored her. On this the bishop writes, that these supernatural graces which God designed to grant to St. Teresa, as he has also done to other saints, are not necessary in order to arrive at sanctity, since many souls have become saints without them; and, on the other hand, many have arrived at sanctity, and yet have, after all, been damned. Therefore he says it is superfluous, and even presumptuous, to desire and to ask for these [Grimm III, 77] ipernatural gifts, when the true and only way to beyme a Saint is, to exercise ourselves in virtue and in the ve of God; and this is done by means of prayer, and y corresponding to the inspirations and assistance of od, who wishes nothing so much as to see us saints. or this is the will of God, your sanctification. Hence Bishop Palafox, speaking of the grades of superatural prayer mentioned in St. Teresa’s letter, namely, 1€ prayer of quiet, the sleep or suspension of the facules, the prayer of union, ecstasy or rapture, flight and ipulse of the spirit, and the wound of love, says, very isely, that as regards the prayer of guiet, what we ought ask of God is that he would free us from attachment to orldly goods, and the desire of them, which give no ace, but bring disquiet and affliction to the soul: anity of vanities, as Solomon well called them, and vexam of spirit.” The heart of man will never find true ‘ace, if it does not empty itself of all that is not God, -as to leave itself all free for his love, that he alone ay possess the whole of it. But this the soul cannot ) of itself; it must obtain it of God by repeated prayers. s regards the sleep and suspension of the faculties, we ight to ask God for grace to keep them asleep for all at is temporal, and only awake them to consider God’s yodness, and to set our hearts upon his love and eter- | happiness. As regards the waion of the faculties, let pray him to give us grace not to think, nor to seek, rr to wish anything but what God wills; since all nctity and the perfection of love consists in uniting ir will to the will of God. As regards ecstasy and rapre, let us pray God to draw us away from the inordite love of ourselves and of creatures, and to draw us tirely to himself. As regards “he flight of the spirit, let pray him to give us grace to live altogether detached “« Heec est enim voluntas Dei, sanctificatio vestra.”—1 Thess. iv. 3. - Universa vanitas et afflictio spiritus.” —Zcc/es. i. 14. [Grimm III, 78] from this world, and to do as the swallows, that do nc settle on the ground even to feed, but take their foo flying;—so should we use our temporal goods for all thé is necessary for the support of life, but always flying without settling on the ground to look for earthly plea ures, As regards impulse of spirit, let us pray him t give us courage and strength to do violence to ourselve whenever it is necessary, for resisting the assaults of ov enemies, for conquering our passions, and for acceptin sufferings even in the midst of desolation and dryness ¢ spirit. Finally, as regards the wound of love, as a woun by its pain perpetually renews the remembrance of whi we suffer, so ought we to pray God to wound ot hearts with his holy love in such a way that we sha always be reminded of his goodness and the love whi¢ he has borne us; and thus we should live in continu: love of him, and should be always pleasing him with ot works and our affections. But none of these graces cé be obtained without prayer; and with prayer, provided be humble, confident, and persevering, everything obtained.

#### FOUNDATION OF OUR CONFIDENCE.

But on what, a man will say, am I, a miserable sinner, found this certain confidence of obtaining what Iask? °° 1 what? On the promise made by Jesus Christ: sf, Z you shall receive." “ Who will fear to be deceived, len the truth promises ?” says St. Augustine.’ How on we doubt that we shall be heard, when God, who is ith itself, promises to give us that which we ask of nin prayer? “We should not be exhorted to ask,” ys the same Father, “unless he meant to give.’ Cernly God would not have exhorted us to ask him for rors, if he had not determined to grant them; but this the very thing to which he exhorts us so strongly, and ich is repeated so often in the Scriptures—pray, ask, :k, and you shall obtain what you desire: Whatever will, seek and it shall be done to you.” And in order that may pray to him with due confidence, our Saviour ; taught us, in the “Our Father,” that when we have course to him for the graces necessary to salvation | of which are included in the petitions of the Lord’s ayer) we should call him, not Lord, but Father—‘“ Our ‘*Qui confidunt in Domino, sicut mons Sion.”—/s. cxxiv. I. “*Quzecumque orantes petitis, credite quia accipietis, et evenient is.”"—Mark, xi. 24. [Grimm III, 84][^3-84-2][^3-84-1] Father’—because it is his will that we should ask Go for grace with the same confidence with which a sor when in want or sick, asks food or medicine from hi own father. If ason is dying of hunger, he has only t make his case known to his father, and his father wi forthwith provide him with food; and if he has receive a bite from a venomous serpent, he has only to show hi father the wound, and the father will immediately appl whatever remedy he has.

Trusting, therefore, in God’s promises, let us alway pray with confidence; not vacillating, but stable and firr as the Apostle says: Let us hold fast the confession of om hope without wavering ; for He is faithful that hath promised As it is perfectly certain that God is faithful in hi promises, so ought our faith also to be perfectly certai that he will hear us when we pray. And although some times, when we are in astate of aridity, or disturbed b some fault we have committed, we perhaps do not fee while praying that sensible confidence which we woul wish to experience, yet, for all this, let us force ou selves to pray, and to pray without ceasing; for Go will not neglect to hear us. Nay, rather he will hear u more readily; because we shall then pray with more di trust of ourselves; and confiding only in the goodnes and faithfulness of God, who has promised to hear th man who prays to him. Oh, how God is pleased in th time of our tribulations, of our fears, and of our tempt tions, to see us hope against hope; that is, in spite of th feeling of diffidence which we then experience becaus of our desolation! This is that for which the Apost praises the patriarch Abraham, who against hope, belie in hope.”

St. John says that he who reposes a sure trust in Go [Grimm III, 85] rtainly will become a saint: And every one that hath this pe in Him sanctifieth himself, as he also is holy» For God ves abundant graces to them that trust in him. For who, says Ecclesiasticus, has ever called on God for aid, and has been neglected and left unaided by him? Or who hath called upon Him, and He hath despised him ?* |St. Augustine says that prayer is a key which opens heaven to us; the same moment in which our prayer asccends to God, the grace which we ask for descends to jus: “The prayer of the just isthe key of heaven; the petition ascends, and the mercy of God descends.” * The royal prophet writes that our supplications and God’s mercy are united together: Blessed is God, who has néi turned away my prayer, nor His mercy for me.” And hence the same St. Augustine says that when we are praying - to God, we ought to be certain that God is hearing us “When you see that your prayer is not removed from you, be sure that his mercy is not removed from you.”* And for myself, I speak the truth, I never fee greater consolation, nor a greater confidence of my salva: tion, than when I am praying to God, and recommend ing myself to him. And I think that the same thing happens to all other believers; for the other signs of ow salvation are uncertain and unstable; but that God hear: the man who prays to him with confidence is an infal lible truth, as it is infallible that God cannot fail in hi promises.

When we find ourselves weak, and unable to overcom any passion, or any great difficulty, so as to fulfil tha [Grimm III, 87] he Confidence with which we ought to Pray. 87 uich God requires of us, let us take courage and say, th the Apostle, 7 can do all things in Him, who strengtheth me. Let us not say, as some do,I cannot; I disist myself. With our own strength certainly we can )nothing; but with God’s help we can do everything. God said to any one, Take this mountain on your back, d carry it, for 1 am helping you, would not the man be mistrustful fool if he answered, I will not take it; for have not strength to carry it? And thus, when we iow how miserable and weak we are, and when we find selves most encompassed with temptations, let us not se heart; but let us lift up our eyes to God, and say, th David, Zhe Lord is my helper; and I will despise my emies.. With the help of my Lord, I shall overcome d laugh to scorn all the assaults of my foes. And 1en we find-ourselves in danger of offending God, or in y other critical position, and are too confused toknow lat is best to be done, let us recommend ourselves to od, saying, The Lord ts my light and my salvation; whom 3 wl I fear?® And let us be sure that God will then rtainly give us light, and will save us from every evil.

#### THE PRAYER OF SINNERS.

But I am a sinner, you will say; and in the Scriptures read, God heareth not sinners.” St. Thomas answers ith St. Augustine) that this was said by the blind man, 10, when he spoke, had not as yet been enlightened: [That is the word of a blind man not yet perfectly lightened, and therefore it is not authoritative.”[^3-87-1] “Omnia possum in eo qui me confortat.”—PA/z/. iv. 13. “‘Dominus mihi adjutor, et ego despiciam inimicos meos.”—Ps. Whisns illuminatio mea et salus mea, quem timebo?’—Ps. iy A [Grimm III, 88][^3-88-3][^3-88-2][^3-88-1]

d 88 The Conditions of Prayer. (PART I. Though, adds St. Thomas, it is true of the petition which the sinner makes, “so far forth as he is a sinner; that is, when he asks from a desire of continuing to sin; as, take revenge on his enemy, or to execute any other a for instance, if he were to ask assistance to enable him t intention. The same holds good for the sinner who prays Ged to save him, but has no desire to quit the state of sin. There are some unhappy persons who love the chains with which the devil keeps them bound like slaves. The prayers of such men are not heard by God¢ because they are rash, presumptuous, and abominable. For what greater presumption can there be than for a man to ask favors of a prince whom he not only has often offended, but whom he intends to offend still more? And this is the meaning of the Holy Spirit, when he says that the prayer of him who turns away his ears s@ as not to hear what God commands is detestable and odious to God: He who turneth away his ears from learning the law, his prayer shall be an abomination... To these peos ple God says, It is of no use your praying to me, for I will turn My eyes from you, and will not hear you. When you stretch forth your hands, J will turn away my eye from you; and when you multiply prayer, I will not hear.. Such, precisely, was the prayer of the impious King An} tiochus, who prayed to God, and made great promisesé but insincerely, and with a heart obstinate in sin; the sole object of his prayer being to escape the punishmen) that impended over him; therefore God did not hear mi prayer, but caused him to die devoured by worms: Zhe this wicked man prayed to the Lord, of whom he was not too tain mercy.” [Grimm III, 89][^3-89-5][^3-89-4][^3-89-3][^3-89-2][^3-89-1] The Confidence with which we ought to Pray. 80

But others, who sin through frailty, or by the violence of some great passion, and who groan under the yoke of the enemy, and desire to break these chains of death, ind to escape from their miserable slavery, and therefore ask the assistance of God; the prayer of these, if it is 9ersevering, will certainly be heard by him, who says that every one that asks receives; and he who seeks yrace finds it: For every one that asketh receiveth, and he hat seeketh findeth." “Every one, whether he be a just man or a sinner,” says the author of the Opus Jmperfecum. And in St. Luke,.our Lord, when speaking of the man who gave all the loaves he had to his friend, not so much on account of his friendship as because of the Mther’s importunity, says, Jf he shall continue knocking, 1 ay to you, although he will not rise and give him because he ts bis friend, yet because of his importunity he will rise and give him as many as he needeth.* And so I say unto you, Ask, and t shall be given you. So that persevering prayer obtains nercy from God, even for those who are not his friends. That which is not obtained through friendship, says St. Shrysostom, is obtained by prayer: “That which was 10t effected by friendship was effected by prayer.” He sven says that prayer is valued more by God than friendhip: “Friendship is not of such avail with God as yrayer; that which is not effected by friendship is ffected by prayer.’ And St. Basil doubts not that even inners obtain what they ask if they persevere in pray- [Grimm III, 90][^3-90-6][^3-90-5][^3-90-4][^3-90-3][^3-90-2][^3-90-1] ing: “Sinners obtain what they seek, if they seek perseveringly.”’ St. Gregory says the same: “The sinner also shall cry, and his prayer shall reach to God.”’* So? St. Jerome,’ who says that even the sinner can call Godhis Father, if he prays to him to receive him anew as a son; after the example of the Prodigal Son, who called him Father, Father, I have sinned,’ even though he had not as yet been pardoned. If God did not hear sinners, says St. Augustine, in vain would the Publican have asked for forgiveness: “If God does not hear sinners, ins vain would that Publican have said, God be merciful to me asinner.”° But the Gospel assures us that the Publican did by his prayer obtain forgiveness: Z/zs man went down to his house justified.

But further still, St. Thomas examines this point more minutely, and does not hesitate to affirm that even the sinner is heard if he prays; for though his prayer is not meritorious, yet it has the power of impetration,—that is, of obtaining what we ask; because impetration is not founded on God’s justice, but on his goodness. ‘“ Merit,” he says, “depends on justice; impetration, on grace.”” Thus did Daniel pray, /ucline, O my God, Thine ear and hear... . For not in our justifications do we present our prayers before Thy face, but in the multitude of Thy mercies.© [Grimm III, 91][^3-91-9][^3-91-8][^3-91-7][^3-91-6][^3-91-5][^3-91-4][^3-91-3][^3-91-2][^3-91-1] therefore, when we pray, says St. Thomas, it is not nec-» ssary to be friends of God, in order to obtain the grace re ask; for prayer itself renders us his friends: “Prayer self makes us of the family of God.”! Moreover, St. ernard uses a beautiful explanation of this, saying that 1e prayer of a sinner to escape from sin arises from the esire to return to the grace of God. Now this desire a gift, which is certainly given by no other than God imself; to what end, therefore, says St. Bernard, would rod give to a sinner this holy desire, unless he meant to ear him? ‘“ For what would he give the desire, unless e willed to hear?’* And, indeed, in the Holy Scripires themselves there are multitudes of instances of siners who have been delivered from sin by prayer. Thus as King Achab®* delivered; thus King Manasses;* thus ing Nabuchodonosor;* and thus the good thief.° Oh, 1e wonderful! ch, the mighty power of prayer! Two nners are dying on Calvary by theside of Jesus Christ: ne, because he prays, “ Remember me,” is saved; the ther, because he prays not, is damned. And, in fine, St. Chrysostom says, “ No man has with yrrow asked favors from him, without obtaining what = wished.” ’ No sinner has ever with penitence prayed » God, without having his desires granted. But why 1ould we cite more authorities, and give more reasons, y demonstrate this point, when our Lord himself says, ame'to Me, all you that labor and are burdened, and T will fresh you.. The “burdened,” according to Saints [Grimm III, 92][^3-92-3][^3-92-2][^3-92-1]

t f 92 The Conditions of Prayer. [PART 1. : Augustine, Jerome, and others, are sinners in general, who groan under the load of their sins; and who, if they. have recourse to God, will surely, according to his promise, be refreshed and saved by his grace. Ah, we cannot desire to be pardoned so much as he longs to pardon us. “Thou dost not,” says St. Chrysostom, “so much desire thy sins to be forgiven, as he desires to forgive thy sins.”’ There is no grace, he goes on to say, that is not obtained by prayer, though it be the prayer of the most abandoned sinner, provided only it be persevering: “ There is nothing which prayer cannot obtain, though a man be guilty of a thousand sins, provided it be fervent and unremitting.” * And let us mark well the words of St. James: /f any man wanteth wisdom, let him ask of God, who giveth to all abundantly, and upbraideth note All those, therefore, who pray to God, are infallibly heard by him, and receive grace in abundance: He giveth to all abundantly. But you should particularly remark the words which follow, and upbraideth not. This means that God does not do as men, who, when a person who has formerly done them an injury comes to ask a favor, immediately upbraid him with his offence. God does not do so to the man who prays, even though he were the greatest sinner in the world, when he asks for some grace conducive to his eternal salvation. Then he does not upbraid him with the offences he has committed: but, as though he had never displeased him, he instantly receives him, he consoles him, he hears him, and enriches him with an abundance of his gifts. Te crown all, our Saviour, in order to encourage us tc [Grimm III, 93][^3-93-2][^3-93-1] ray, says, Amen, amen, I say to you, tf you ask the Father nything in My name, He will give tt you.’ As though he ad said, Courage, O sinners; do not despair: do not let our sins turn you away from having recourse to My ‘ather, and from hoping to be'saved by him, if you deire it. You have not now any merits to obtain the traces which you ask for, for you only deserve to be unished; still do this: go to My Father in My name, hrough My merits ask the favors which you want, and I romise and swear to you (“ Amen, amen, I say to you,” rhich, according to St. Augustine, is a species of oath) hat whatever you ask, My Father will grant. O God, that greater comfort can a sinner have after his fall nan to know for certain that all that he asks from God 1 the name of Jesus Christ will be given to him! I say “all;” but I mean only that which has reference > his eternal salvation; for with respect to temporal oods, we have already shown that God, even when sked, sometimes does not give them; because he sees ° aat they would injure our soul. But so far as relates ) spiritual goods, his promise to hear us is not condional, but absolute; and therefore St. Augustine tells s, that those things which God promises absolutely, re should demand with absolute certainty of receiving: Those things which God promises, seek with certainty.” * nd how, says the saint, can God ever deny us anything, then we ask him for it with confidence? how much ore does he desire to dispense to us his graces, than e to receive them! “He is more willing to be munifient of his benefits to thee than thou art desirous to sceive them.” * St. Chrysostom says that the only time when God is [Grimm III, 94][^3-94-3][^3-94-2][^3-94-1] angry with us is when we neglect to ask him for his gifts: “He is only angry when we do not pray.”* And, \ , how can it ever happen that God will not hear a soul who asks him for favors all according to his pleasure? When the soul says to him, Lord, I ask Thee not for goods of this world,—riches, pleasures, honors; I ask Thee only for Thy grace: deliver me from sin, grant me a good death, give me Paradise, give me Thy holy love (which is that grace which St. Francis de Sales says we should seek more than all others), give me resignation to Thy will; how is it possible that God should not hear! What petitions wilt Thou, O my God, ever hear (says St. Augustine), if Thou dost not hear those which are made after Thy own heart? “What prayers dost Thou hear, if Thou hearest not these ?’* But, above all, our confidence ought to revive, when we pray to God for spiritual graces, as Jesus Christ says: // you, being evil, know how to give good gifts to your children, how much more will your father from heaven give the good Spirit to them that ask Him!* Vf you, who are so attached to your own interests, so full of self-love, cannot refuse your children that which they ask, how much more will your heavenly Father, who loves you better than any earthly father, grant you his spiritual goods when you pray for them!

#### 4. {#alfonso-gran-mezzo.1.3.4}

**The Perseverance Required in Prayer.**

Our prayers, then, must be humble and confident ; but this is not enough to obtain final perseverance, and thereby eternal life. Individual prayers will obtain the indi- [Grimm III, 95][^3-95-1] vidual graces which they ask of God ; but unless they are persevering, they will not obtain final perseverance: which, as it is the accumulation of many graces, requires many prayers, that are not tocease tilldeath. Thegrace of salvation is not a single grace, but a chain of graces, all of which are at last linked with the grace of final perseverance. Now, to this chain of graces there ought to sorrespond another chain (as it were) of our prayers; if we, by neglecting to pray, break the chain of our prayers, the chain of graces will be broken too; and as it is by this that we have to obtain salvation, we shall not be saved.

It is true that we cannot merit final perseverance, as the Council of Trent teaches: “It cannot be had from any other source but from him whois able to confirm the man who is standing, that he may stand with perseverance.” * Nevertheless, says St. Augustine, this great gift of perseverance can ina manner be merited by our praysrs; that is, can be obtained by praying: “This gift, therefore, can besuppliantly merited; that is, can be obained by supplication.”* And F. Suarez adds, that the man who prays, infallibly obtains it. But to obtain it, ind to save ourselves, says St. Thomas, a persevering and sontinual prayer is necessary: “ After baptism continual orayer is necessary to a man in order that he may enter 1eaven.”* And before this, our Saviour himself had said it over and over again: We ought always to pray, and not to faint. Watch ye therefore, praying at all times, that ou may be accounted worthy to escape all these things that are [Grimm III, 96][^3-96-6][^3-96-5][^3-96-4][^3-96-3][^3-96-2][^3-96-1] to come, and to stand before the Son of man." Thesame had been previously said in the Old Testament : Let nothing. hinder thee from praying always.” Bless God at all times, and desire Him to direct thy ways.’ Wence the Apostle inculcated on his disciples never to neglect prayer: Pray without intermission.’ Be instant in prayer, watching tn it with thanksgiving.’ I will therefore that men pray in every place.’ God does indeed wish to give us perseverance, says St. Nilus, but he will only give it to him who prays for it perseveringly : ‘““He willeth to confer benefits on him who perseveres in prayer.’’’ Many sinners by the help of God’s grace come to be converted, and to receive pardon. But then, because they neglect to ask for perseverance, they fall again, and lose all.

Nor is it enough, says Bellarmine, to ask the grace of perseverance once, or a few times; we ought always to ask it, every day till our death, if we wish to obtain it: “It must be asked day by day, that it may be obtained day by day.”"* He who asks it one day, obtains it for that one day; but if he does not ask it the next day, the next day he will fall.

And this isthe lesson which our Lord wished to teach us in the parable of the man who would not give his loaves to his friend who asked him for them until he had become importunate in his demand: <4/though he will not [Grimm III, 97][^3-97-3][^3-97-2][^3-97-1] rise and give because he is his friend, yet because of his importunity, he will rise and give him as many as he needeth> Now if this man, solely to deliver himself from the troublesome importunity of his friend, gave him even against his own will the loaves for which he asked, “how much more,” says St. Augustine, “ will the good God give, who both commands us to ask, and is angry if we ask not!’”’? How much more will God, who, as he is infinite goodness, has a commensurate desire to communicate to us his good things,—how much more will he give his graces when we ask him for them! And the more, as he himself tells us to ask for them, and as he is displeased when we do not demand them. God, then, does indeed wish to give us eternal life, and therein all graces; but he wishes also that we should never omit to ask him for them, even to the extent of being troublesome. Cornelius 4 Lapide says on the text just quoted, “‘God wishes us to be persevering in prayer to the extent of importunity.”* Men of the world cannot bear the importunate; but God not only bears with them, but wishes us to be importunate in praying to him for graces, and especially for perseverance. St. Gregory says that God wishes us to do him violence by our prayers; for such violence does not annoy, but pleases him: “God wills to be called upon, he wills to be forced, he wills to be conquered by importunity. . . . Happy violence, by which God is not offended, but appeased !” * [Grimm III, 98][^3-98-5][^3-98-4][^3-98-3][^3-98-2][^3-98-1]

So that to obtain perseverance we must always recommend ourselves to God, morning and night, at medita-, tion, at Mass, at Communion, and always; especially in time of temptation, when we must keep repeating, Lord, help me; Lord, assist me; keep Thy hand upon me; leave me not; have pity upon me! Is there anything casier than to say, Lord, help me, assist me! The Psalmist says, With me is prayer to the God of my Ufe. On which the gloss is as follows: “ A man may say, I cannot fast, I cannot give alms; but if he is told to pray, he cannot say this.” Because there is nothing easier than to pray. But we must never cease praying; we must (so to speak) continually do violence to God, that he may assist us always—a violence which 1s delightful and dear to him.” “This violence is grateful to God,” says Tertullian;* and St. Jerome says that the more persevering and importunate our prayers are, so much the more are they acceptable to God: “ Prayer, as long as it is importunate, is more acceptable.” *

Blessed is the man that heareth me, and that watcheth daily at my gates.’ Happy is that man, says God, who listens to Me, and watches continually with holy prayers at the gates of My mercy And Isaias says, Blessed are all they that wait for Him.’ Blessed are they who till the end wait (in prayer) for their salvation from God. Therefore inthe Gospel Jesus Christ exhorts us to pray; but how? Ask, and ye shall receive; seek, and ye shall find; [Grimm III, 99][^3-99-1] mock, and it shall be opened to you. Would it not have 9€en enough to have said, “ask?” why add “seek” and ‘knock?” No, it was not superfluous to add them; for hereby our Saviour wished us to understand that we yught to do as the poor who go begging. If they do not eceive the alms they ask (I speak of licensed beggars), hey do not cease asking; they return to ask again; and f the master of the house does not show himself any nore, they set to work to knock at the door, till they be- -ome very importunate and troublesome. That is what sod wishes us to do: to pray, and to pray again, and 1ever leave off praying, that he would assist us and sucor us, that he would enlighten us and strengthen us, ind never allow us to forfeit his grace. The learned uessius says’ that the man cannot be excused from nortal sin who does not pray when he is in sin, or in langer of death; or, again, if he neglects to pray for any 1otable time, as (he says) for one or two months. But his does not include the time of temptations; because vhoever finds himself assailed by any grievous temptaion, without doubt sins mortally if he does not have recourse to God in prayer, to ask for assistance to resist t; seeing that otherwise he places himself in a proxinate, nay, in a certain, occasion of sin. VHY GOD DELAYS GRANTING US FINAL PERSEVERANCE,.—

f CONCLUSION. But, some one will say, since God can give and wishes o give me the grace of perseverance, why does he not ‘ive it me all at once, when I ask him? The holy Fathers assign many reasons: 1. God does not grant it at once, but delays it, first, hat he may better prove our confidence. [Grimm III, 100][^3-100-2][^3-100-1]

2. And, further, says St. Augustine, that we may long for it'midre vehemently. Great gifts, he says, should be greatly desired; for good things soon obtained are no held in the same estimation as those which have beet long looked for: “God wills not to give quickly, tha you may learn to have great desire for great things things long desired are pleasanter to obtain, but thing: soon given are cheapened.”*

3. Again, the Lord does so that we may not forge him; if we were already secure of persevering and o being saved, and if we had not continual need of God’ help to preserve us in his grace and to save us, we shoulk soon forget God. Want makes the poor keep resorting to the houses of the rich; so God, to draw us to himself as St. Chrysostom says, and to see us often at his feet in order that he may thus be able to do.us greater good delays giving us the complete grace of salvation till th hour of our death: “It is not because he rejects ou prayers that he delays, but by this contrivance he wishe to make us careful, and to draw us to himself.”* Again he does so in order that we, by persevering in prayet may unite ourselves closer to him with the sweet bond of love: “Prayer,” says the same St. Chrysostom ‘““which is accustomed to converse with God, is no sligh bond of love to him.”* This continual recurrence t God in prayer, and this confident expectation of th graces which we desire from him, oh, what a great spu and chain is it of love to inflame us, and to bind us mor closely to God! [Grimm III, 101][^3-101-6][^3-101-5][^3-101-4][^3-101-3][^3-101-2][^3-101-1] ae le

But, till what time have we to pr 25 Always; says J the same saint, till we receive favorable § édoendf, eter~ nal life; that is to say, till our death: “Do not Téave off ill you receive.” * And he goes on to say that the man who resolves, I will never leave off praying till I am saved, will most certainly be saved: “If you say, I will n0t give in till I have received, you will assuredly reseive.”* The Apostle writes that many run for the prize, out that he only receives it who runs till he wins: Know jou not that they who run in the race, all run indeed, but one recetveth the prize? So run that you may obtain.* It is 10t, then, enough for salvation simply to pray; but we nust pray always, that we may come to receive the srown which God promises, but promises only to those who are constant in prayer till the end. So that if we wish to be saved, we must do as David lid, who always kept his eyes turned to God, to implore is aid against being overcome by his enemies: J/y eyes wre ever towards the Lord, for He shall pluck my feet out of he snare." As the devil does not cease continually preading snares to swallow us up, as St. Peter writes: Vour adversary the devil, as a roaring lion, goeth about, seekng whom he may devour; so ought we ever to stand with yur arms in our hands to defend ourselves from sucha oe, and to say, with the royal prophet, / w7// pursue after ny enemies; and I will not turn again till they are consumed." [Grimm III, 102][^3-102-5][^3-102-4][^3-102-3][^3-102-2][^3-102-1] 102 The Conditions of Prayer. [PART L I will never cease fighting till I see my enemies conquered. But how can we obtain this victory, so impor: tant for us, and so difficult? “By most perseverin prayers,’* says St. Augustine,—only by prayers, anc those most persevering; and till when? As long as the fight shall last. ‘As the battle is never over,” says St Bonaventure, “so let us never give over asking fo: mercy.” * As we must be always in the combat, s¢ should we bealways asking God foraid not to be overcome Woe, says the Wise Man, to him who in this battle leave: off praying: Woe to them that have lost patience.“ We may be saved, the Apostle tells us, but on this condition, g we retain a firm confidence and the glory of hope until the end; if we are constant in praying with confidence unti death,

Let us, then, take courage from the mercy of God, ang his promises, and say with the same Apostle, Who then shal separate us from the love of Christ? Shall tribulation, @. distress, or danger, or persecution, or the sword? Who shal succeed in estranging us from the love of Jesus Christ Tribulation, perhaps, or the danger of losing the good of this world? The persecutions of devilsormen? Th torments inflicted by tyrants? Jn all these we overcom (it is St. Paul who encourages us), decause of Him thé hath loved us.“ No, he says, no tribulation, no misery danger, persecution, or torture, shall ever be able to sep arate us from the love of Jesus Christ; because with God’ [Grimm III, 103][^3-103-2] 1elp we shall overcome all, if we fight for love of him Who gave his life for us. F. Hippolitus Durazzo, the day when he resolved to elinquish his dignity of prelate at Rome, and to give limself entirely to God by entering the Society of Jesus which he afterwards did), was so afraid of being faithess by reason of his weakness that he said to God, ‘Forsake me not, Lord, now that I ‘have given myself wholly to Thee; for pity’s sake, donot forsake me!” But te heard the whisper of God in his heart, “Do not thou orsake Me; rather,” said God, “dol say tothee, Forsake Me not.”” And so at last the servant of God, trusting in Ms goodness and help, concluded, “Then, O my God, Thou wilt not leave me,* and I will not leave Thee.”? Finally, if we wish not to be forsaken by God, we yught never to forsake praying to him not to leave us. f we do thus, he will certainly always assist us, and will ever allow us to perish, and to be separated from his ove. And to this end let us not only take care always o ask for final perseverance, and the graces necessary to ybtain it, but let us, at the same time, always by anticipaion ask God for grace to go on praying; for this is pre- ‘isely that great gift which he promised to his elect by he mouth of the prophet: Avd 7 will pour out upon the use of David, and upon the inhabitants of Jerusalem, the pirit of grace and prayers.” Oh, what a great grace is the pirit of prayer; that is, the grace which God confers on .soul to enable it to pray always! Let us, then, never leglect to beg God to give us this grace, and this spirit f continual prayer; because if we pray always, we shall ertainly obtain from God perseverance and every other rift which we desire, since his a of hearing who- [^3-103-1] «Non me deseras,” [Grimm III, 104] ever prays to him cannot fail. For we are saved by hope.” With this hope of always praying, we may reckon our selves saved. ‘Confidence will give us a broad entrance into this city.”? This hope, said Venerable Bede, wi give us a safe passage into the city of Paradise.
